People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well... I guess the main thing is work. I’m a cashier, and lately I’ve been getting stuck at the register doing things that don’t really fit what’s happening. Like, I’ll be in the middle of ringing someone up, and instead of just scanning the next item or opening the drawer, I’ll start tapping the scanner in a certain pattern. Not because it helps or anything — it just sort of happens, and then I feel like I have to finish it.

Or I’ll freeze with my hand over the cash drawer while the customer is standing there waiting, and I know they’re waiting, I can see the line, but my hand just kind of stays there. It’s embarrassing. I can feel people looking at me, and then it makes it harder to move normally.

Last week my manager wrote me up for it, because it happened during a rush and it slowed everything down. So that’s why I came in. I don’t really know how to explain it without sounding strange, but it’s starting to cause problems.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s not exactly like I’m thinking, “If I don’t do this, something bad will happen,” if that’s what you mean. It doesn’t feel like a fear in a clear way.

It’s more like I’m at the register and I’m supposed to just keep going — scan the next item, hit the button, open the drawer — but then I get stuck in some other action that doesn’t really match the moment. Like tapping the scanner in a pattern, and I know it’s not helping the customer or the transaction, but I keep doing it until it feels… finished, I guess. Or my hand just stays over the cash drawer, and I can see the person waiting and I know I should move, but I’m sort of frozen there.

There is tension, but it’s hard to describe. It’s not like a normal anxious thought first. It’s more like my body or my attention gets caught, and then the longer people are looking at me, the worse it gets because I’m aware how strange it must look. That’s what got me written up last week — it happened during a rush and I slowed everything down.

It’s not always exactly the same, no. I mean, it has a pattern to it in the moment — like I’ll tap the scanner a certain way and then it feels like I’m supposed to complete that little sequence — but I don’t think it’s the same exact rhythm every single time.

Sometimes it’s more like tap-tap, pause, tap, or I’ll do it with the side of my fingers, and other times it’s just… I don’t know, my hand gets caught doing something that doesn’t belong with what I’m supposed to be doing. The part that bothers me is less the exact pattern and more that I’m at the register, with a customer standing there, and instead of scanning or opening the drawer like a normal person would, I’m doing this extra thing or I just freeze with my hand over the cash drawer.

And I know it doesn’t fit the situation. That’s what’s so embarrassing. I can see the line, I can see the customer waiting, and I’m aware I’m holding everything up, but I still get stuck there. Last week it happened badly enough during a rush that my manager wrote me up, so now I’m even more aware of it when I’m standing there.

I don’t really know how to explain it without sounding strange, but it’s starting to cause problems.\n\nDoctor: Right before you start tapping the scanner or freezing your hand, do you notice an anxious thought or fear driving you to do it, or is it more like a physical urge or tension that builds up until you do the movement?\n\nPatient: It’s not exactly like I’m thinking, “If I don’t do this, something bad will happen,” if that’s what you mean. It doesn’t feel like a fear in a clear way.\n\nIt’s more like I’m at the register and I’m supposed to just keep going — scan the next item, hit the button, open the drawer — but then I get stuck in some other action that doesn’t really match the moment. Like tapping the scanner in a pattern, and I know it’s not helping the customer or the transaction, but I keep doing it until it feels… finished, I guess. Or my hand just stays over the cash drawer, and I can see the person waiting and I know I should move, but I’m sort of frozen there.\n\nThere is tension, but it’s hard to describe. It’s not like a normal anxious thought first. It’s more like my body or my attention gets caught, and then the longer people are looking at me, the worse it gets because I’m aware how strange it must look. That’s what got me written up last week — it happened during a rush and I slowed everything down.\n\nDoctor: When you tap the scanner, is it always the exact same rhythmic pattern every time, or does the specific pattern change depending on the situation?\n\nPatient: It’s not always exactly the same, no. I mean, it has a pattern to it in the moment — like I’ll tap the scanner a certain way and then it feels like I’m supposed to complete that little sequence — but I don’t think it’s the same exact rhythm every single time.\n\nSometimes it’s more like tap-tap, pause, tap, or I’ll do it with the side of my fingers, and other times it’s just… I don’t know, my hand gets caught doing something that doesn’t belong with what I’m supposed to be doing. The part that bothers me is less the exact pattern and more that I’m at the register, with a customer standing there, and instead of scanning or opening the drawer like a normal person would, I’m doing this extra thing or I just freeze with my hand over the cash drawer.\n\nAnd I know it doesn’t fit the situation. That’s what’s so embarrassing. I can see the line, I can see the customer waiting, and I’m aware I’m holding everything up, but I still get stuck there. Last week it happened badly enough during a rush that my manager wrote me up, so now I’m even more aware of it when I’m standing there.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you tell me when these tapping or freezing behaviors first started — did you notice anything like this back in childhood, or is this something that began more recently as an adult?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

It’s more recent. I don’t remember doing anything like this as a kid, not in the way it’s happening now. I mean, I had little habits like anyone, I guess, but not getting stuck at a register or freezing in the middle of doing something with people waiting.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more like actual voices, not just my own thoughts being loud. I mean, I’ve had thoughts race around before, like anybody, but this feels different. It sounds like people are just off to the side of me, like maybe two people nearby talking, and I’ll turn my head because I honestly think someone said something.

Most days it happens at least some. Sometimes I hear my name, like from the stockroom or from behind me, and I’ll think somebody needs me or a coworker is calling me. But then I go look or I turn around and there’s nobody there, or the person nearby says they didn’t say anything.

Other times it’s more tied to work, like I’ll hear someone say I rang something up wrong, or that I missed an item, and it sounds close enough that I’ll stop and check the screen or look around. It’s embarrassing because I can’t tell right away if it’s real. It doesn’t feel like I’m imagining a thought in my head — it feels like I heard it with my ears, if that makes sense. Then when nobody else reacts, that’s when I realize maybe it was just me hearing it.

The part that bothers me is less the exact pattern and more that I’m at the register, with a customer standing there, and instead of scanning or opening the drawer like a normal person would, I’m doing this extra thing or I just freeze with my hand over the cash drawer.\n\nAnd I know it doesn’t fit the situation. That’s what’s so embarrassing. I can see the line, I can see the customer waiting, and I’m aware I’m holding everything up, but I still get stuck there. Last week it happened badly enough during a rush that my manager wrote me up, so now I’m even more aware of it when I’m standing there.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me when these tapping or freezing behaviors first started — did you notice anything like this back in childhood, or is this something that began more recently as an adult?\n\nPatient: It’s more recent. I don’t remember doing anything like this as a kid, not in the way it’s happening now. I mean, I had little habits like anyone, I guess, but not getting stuck at a register or freezing in the middle of doing something with people waiting.\n\nThe tapping and the really out-of-place moments have been going on for about the last three months, maybe around there. That’s when I started noticing it wasn’t just being distracted or nervous — it was happening enough that it felt like something was off. And around that same time, the voices started too, so… I don’t know if that’s connected, but it’s been roughly that same stretch, about three months.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about these voices you mentioned—are they clearly voices talking to or about you that other people can't hear, or something more like your own thoughts feeling unusually loud?\n\nPatient: It’s more like actual voices, not just my own thoughts being loud. I mean, I’ve had thoughts race around before, like anybody, but this feels different. It sounds like people are just off to the side of me, like maybe two people nearby talking, and I’ll turn my head because I honestly think someone said something.\n\nMost days it happens at least some. Sometimes I hear my name, like from the stockroom or from behind me, and I’ll think somebody needs me or a coworker is calling me. But then I go look or I turn around and there’s nobody there, or the person nearby says they didn’t say anything.\n\nOther times it’s more tied to work, like I’ll hear someone say I rang something up wrong, or that I missed an item, and it sounds close enough that I’ll stop and check the screen or look around. It’s embarrassing because I can’t tell right away if it’s real. It doesn’t feel like I’m imagining a thought in my head — it feels like I heard it with my ears, if that makes sense. Then when nobody else reacts, that’s when I realize maybe it was just me hearing it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had any recent illnesses, head injury, new medications, or use of drugs or alcohol around the time these symptoms started three months ago?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / cashier / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. I’m not really sure. I’ve been trying to think back over that, because it would make more sense to me if there was some obvious thing I could point to, like, “Oh, that happened and then everything changed.” But I don’t have a clear answer like that.

I don’t remember hitting my head or having some accident around then. I wasn’t, like, in a car crash or anything that stands out. As far as being sick, I mean, I get normal colds and feeling run down sometimes, but I don’t remember some big illness right before this started.

With medications, I’d have to think through dates because I’m not great at remembering exactly when things changed, but nothing is jumping out to me as, “I started this new pill and then the tapping and all that started.” And drugs or alcohol… I’m not connecting it to anything like that either. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think they’ve gone away completely for any real stretch. I mean, it’s not like every minute of every day I’m hearing something, but since it started, around three months ago, it’s been sort of… there. Like some days are quieter or I can ignore it better, and then other days it feels much more obvious and I’m checking around me more.

I can’t think of a week where I felt totally normal again, like, “Oh good, that whole thing stopped.” The voices and those more clearly out-of-place moments have been happening through this whole time, off and on, but not really disappearing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still care about my wife and my kids, so it’s not like I don’t love them or anything. That’s part of what makes it feel strange, because the feeling is still there somewhere, but the… the push to actually do things seems lower.

Like my son’s away at college, and I’ll think, “I should call him,” and then I just don’t. Not because I don’t want to hear his voice, exactly. It’s more like even picking up the phone feels like this whole task, and then I put it off, and then I feel bad about it, and then it’s even harder to do. Same with the kayak group I used to meet up with. I used to look forward to that, getting out on the water and joking around with the guys, but lately I just don’t make myself go. I’ll tell myself maybe next time, and then next time comes and I still don’t.

Even around the house, I have these little repair projects—things I normally would’ve liked messing with, fixing a hinge or working on something small—and now they just sit there. I look at them and know what needs doing, but I don’t seem to start.

And when people ask me what’s wrong, I mostly just shrug. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude. I just can’t find much to say. It’s like the words aren’t there, or I don’t have the energy to explain it.

That’s part of what makes it feel strange, because the feeling is still there somewhere, but the… the push to actually do things seems lower.\n\nLike my son’s away at college, and I’ll think, “I should call him,” and then I just don’t. Not because I don’t want to hear his voice, exactly. It’s more like even picking up the phone feels like this whole task, and then I put it off, and then I feel bad about it, and then it’s even harder to do. Same with the kayak group I used to meet up with. I used to look forward to that, getting out on the water and joking around with the guys, but lately I just don’t make myself go. I’ll tell myself maybe next time, and then next time comes and I still don’t.\n\nEven around the house, I have these little repair projects—things I normally would’ve liked messing with, fixing a hinge or working on something small—and now they just sit there. I look at them and know what needs doing, but I don’t seem to start.\n\nAnd when people ask me what’s wrong, I mostly just shrug. It’s not that I’m trying to be rude. I just can’t find much to say. It’s like the words aren’t there, or I don’t have the energy to explain it."
  }

Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking. Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nIt’s not exactly like I’m thinking, “If I don’t do this, something bad will happen,” if that’s what you mean. It doesn’t feel like a fear in a clear way.\n\nIt’s more like I’m at the register and I’m supposed to just keep going — scan the next item, hit the button, open the drawer — but then I get stuck in some other action that doesn’t really match the moment. Like tapping the scanner in a pattern, and I know it’s not helping the customer or the transaction, but I keep doing it until it feels… finished, I guess. Or my hand just stays over the cash drawer, and I can see the person waiting and I know I should move, but I’m sort of frozen there.\n\nThere is tension, but it’s hard to describe. It’s not like a normal anxious thought first. It’s more like my body or my attention gets caught, and then the longer people are looking at me, the worse it gets because I’m aware how strange it must look. That’s what got me written up last week — it happened during a rush and I slowed everything down.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"psychotic_symptoms_duration\",\"name\":\"Psychotic Symptoms Duration\",\"description\":\"3 months\",\"reason\":\"The question asks about when the tapping or freezing behaviors first started; this item covers the duration of the more clearly out-of-place psychotic behaviors.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.   }

Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.
